I guess the biggest reason I’m here is my sleep has gotten really unreliable. Like, I’ll be completely exhausted by the end of the day and think, “Okay, I’m definitely going to pass out tonight,” but then I get into bed and my mind just… starts running. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. I mean, I’m still getting through the day, so it’s not like I’ve completely fallen apart, but it’s started to affect things in ways I can’t really ignore anymore.

At work, I’m second-guessing myself constantly. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my day is decisions, forecasts, pipeline reviews, talking through risks with the team, and I used to be able to trust my read on things more. Now I’ll go into a pipeline review and I’ve already mentally replayed the numbers a dozen times, but I’m still questioning whether I’m missing something or if I should have pushed harder on a deal or if I’m about to make the wrong call. And I think the team can feel that tension from me. Those meetings have gotten more strained because I’m not just reviewing the pipeline, I’m sort of… interrogating everything.

And then by the time I get home, I feel completely used up. My husband will be trying to talk to me or just have a normal evening, and I’m physically there, but I’m not really present. I’m tired and short, and I react to small things more than I want to. Then I feel bad about that too, which just gives me more to think about later when I’m trying to sleep. So yes, it’s affecting my energy, but also my patience and how available I am to people.

Yes. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.

And it jumps around so fast. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.

It’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.

It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.

Most days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.

It’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.

I can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.

So yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.

So yes, it’s affecting my energy, but also my patience and how available I am to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your mind starts racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.\n\nAnd it jumps around so fast. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.

It’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.

During the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.

At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.

It feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.

So the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.

It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.\n\nIt feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.\n\nSo the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.

But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. Since then, the nighttime worrying and waking up has just become more and more baked into my routine.

Now I’ll go into a pipeline review and I’ve already mentally replayed the numbers a dozen times, but I’m still questioning whether I’m missing something or if I should have pushed harder on a deal or if I’m about to make the wrong call. And I think the team can feel that tension from me. Those meetings have gotten more strained because I’m not just reviewing the pipeline, I’m sort of… interrogating everything.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I feel completely used up. My husband will be trying to talk to me or just have a normal evening, and I’m physically there, but I’m not really present. I’m tired and short, and I react to small things more than I want to. Then I feel bad about that too, which just gives me more to think about later when I’m trying to sleep. So yes, it’s affecting my energy, but also my patience and how available I am to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your mind starts racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.\n\nAnd it jumps around so fast. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.\n\nIt feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.\n\nSo the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in your life that you think might be triggering these nighttime worries and wake-ups, or does it seem unrelated to any specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stress, like everyone was unsettled and there were new reporting lines and expectations, and I figured once things settled down I’d settle down too. But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. Since then, the nighttime worrying and waking up has just become more and more baked into my routine.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels connected to that reorganization and everything that shifted after it. I don’t know if that’s the whole explanation, but that’s when I can trace it back to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F41.1", "F51.0", "G47.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think about it every single second in those exact words — “the reorganization” — but it feels like everything has been building from that point. It was about thirteen months ago now, and at the time I thought it was temporary, like once the new structure became normal, I’d feel normal again too.

But it hasn’t really felt that way. It’s like the stress from that period never fully let up, and instead it sort of accumulated. So yes, I’d say it’s still significant, even if it’s less like one dramatic event and more like this ongoing thing that started back then and just kept growing.

I’m replaying sales forecasts, conversations with my team, things I should have said differently, issues I need to fix, all of it.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and it’s like my brain turns back on immediately. I can’t get it to power down again, even though I know I need to sleep. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to feel like I can’t count on getting a normal night’s rest anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind racing and sleep issues are affecting your mood or energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I’m still getting through the day, so it’s not like I’ve completely fallen apart, but it’s started to affect things in ways I can’t really ignore anymore.\n\nAt work, I’m second-guessing myself constantly. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my day is decisions, forecasts, pipeline reviews, talking through risks with the team, and I used to be able to trust my read on things more. Now I’ll go into a pipeline review and I’ve already mentally replayed the numbers a dozen times, but I’m still questioning whether I’m missing something or if I should have pushed harder on a deal or if I’m about to make the wrong call. And I think the team can feel that tension from me. Those meetings have gotten more strained because I’m not just reviewing the pipeline, I’m sort of… interrogating everything.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I feel completely used up. My husband will be trying to talk to me or just have a normal evening, and I’m physically there, but I’m not really present. I’m tired and short, and I react to small things more than I want to. Then I feel bad about that too, which just gives me more to think about later when I’m trying to sleep. So yes, it’s affecting my energy, but also my patience and how available I am to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your mind starts racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.\n\nAnd it jumps around so fast. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in your life that you think might be triggering these nighttime worries and wake-ups, or does it seem unrelated to any specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stress, like everyone was unsettled and there were new reporting lines and expectations, and I figured once things settled down I’d settle down too. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stress, like everyone was unsettled and there were new reporting lines and expectations, and I figured once things settled down I’d settle down too. But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. Since then, the nighttime worrying and waking up has just become more and more baked into my routine.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels connected to that reorganization and everything that shifted after it. I don’t know if that’s the whole explanation, but that’s when I can trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sales division reorganization is still causing you significant stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think about it every single second in those exact words — “the reorganization” — but it feels like everything has been building from that point. It was about thirteen months ago now, and at the time I thought it was temporary, like once the new structure became normal, I’d feel normal again too.\n\nBut it hasn’t really felt that way. It’s like the stress from that period never fully let up, and instead it sort of accumulated. So yes, I’d say it’s still significant, even if it’s less like one dramatic event and more like this ongoing thing that started back then and just kept growing.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the stress from the sales division reorganization still the main thing that's on your mind when you're lying awake at night?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"Most days my mind jumps from whether we'll hit the launch numbers to whether someone on my team is about to quit, then to the mortgage or a client renewal, and it feels like every small delay could turn into something major.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s part of it, but honestly, it’s not just the reorganization itself anymore. When I’m lying there awake, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next before I even realize it’s happening.

Like I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, and then that turns into worrying that maybe I missed something in the forecast, and then suddenly I’m thinking about someone on my team seeming quieter than usual and wondering if they’re about to quit. And then somehow it’s the mortgage, or whether a client renewal is going to fall through, or some email I didn’t answer fast enough.

It’s like everything feels connected and important at 3 in the morning. Even a small delay or something that should be manageable starts feeling like it could turn into a much bigger problem. So I guess the reorganization is still the backdrop, but at night it’s more like my brain uses that as the starting point and then runs through every possible thing that could go wrong.

I can’t get it to power down again, even though I know I need to sleep. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to feel like I can’t count on getting a normal night’s rest anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind racing and sleep issues are affecting your mood or energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I’m still getting through the day, so it’s not like I’ve completely fallen apart, but it’s started to affect things in ways I can’t really ignore anymore.\n\nAt work, I’m second-guessing myself constantly. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my day is decisions, forecasts, pipeline reviews, talking through risks with the team, and I used to be able to trust my read on things more. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think sad or depressed is usually the main feeling, no. I mean, I’m not waking up and crying or feeling hopeless in that moment, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more like… I wake up and I’m immediately frustrated and tense because I can tell my brain is already starting up again. Sometimes I do feel kind of low in the sense of, “Great, here we go again, I’m going to be exhausted tomorrow,” but I wouldn’t describe it as this deep sadness. It’s more discouraging, maybe, and tiring.

I guess there are moments where I feel worn down by it, because when it keeps happening night after night it’s hard not to feel defeated. But when I first wake up, the main thing is usually worry and irritation that I can’t just turn it off and go back to sleep.

I’m replaying sales forecasts, conversations with my team, things I should have said differently, issues I need to fix, all of it.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and it’s like my brain turns back on immediately. I can’t get it to power down again, even though I know I need to sleep. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to feel like I can’t count on getting a normal night’s rest anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind racing and sleep issues are affecting your mood or energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I’m still getting through the day, so it’s not like I’ve completely fallen apart, but it’s started to affect things in ways I can’t really ignore anymore.\n\nAt work, I’m second-guessing myself constantly. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my day is decisions, forecasts, pipeline reviews, talking through risks with the team, and I used to be able to trust my read on things more. Now I’ll go into a pipeline review and I’ve already mentally replayed the numbers a dozen times, but I’m still questioning whether I’m missing something or if I should have pushed harder on a deal or if I’m about to make the wrong call. Those meetings have gotten more strained because I’m not just reviewing the pipeline, I’m sort of… interrogating everything.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I feel completely used up. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.\n\nAnd it jumps around so fast. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.\n\nIt feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.\n\nSo the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in your life that you think might be triggering these nighttime worries and wake-ups, or does it seem unrelated to any specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.\n\nIt feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.\n\nSo the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in your life that you think might be triggering these nighttime worries and wake-ups, or does it seem unrelated to any specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stress, like everyone was unsettled and there were new reporting lines and expectations, and I figured once things settled down I’d settle down too. But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. Since then, the nighttime worrying and waking up has just become more and more baked into my routine.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels connected to that reorganization and everything that shifted after it. I don’t know if that’s the whole explanation, but that’s when I can trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sales division reorganization is still causing you significant stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It was about thirteen months ago now, and at the time I thought it was temporary, like once the new structure became normal, I’d feel normal again too.\n\nBut it hasn’t really felt that way. It’s like the stress from that period never fully let up, and instead it sort of accumulated. So yes, I’d say it’s still significant, even if it’s less like one dramatic event and more like this ongoing thing that started back then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress from the sales division reorganization still the main thing that's on your mind when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: It’s part of it, but honestly, it’s not just the reorganization itself anymore. When I’m lying there awake, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next before I even realize it’s happening.\n\nLike I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, and then that turns into worrying that maybe I missed something in the forecast, and then suddenly I’m thinking about someone on my team seeming quieter than usual and wondering if they’re about to quit. And then somehow it’s the mortgage, or whether a client renewal is going to fall through, or some email I didn’t answer fast enough.\n\nIt’s like everything feels connected and important at 3 in the morning. Even a small delay or something that should be manageable starts feeling like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I mean, I’m not waking up and crying or feeling hopeless in that moment, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… I wake up and I’m immediately frustrated and tense because I can tell my brain is already starting up again. Sometimes I do feel kind of low in the sense of, “Great, here we go again, I’m going to be exhausted tomorrow,” but I wouldn’t describe it as this deep sadness. It’s more discouraging, maybe, and tiring.\n\nI guess there are moments where I feel worn down by it, because when it keeps happening night after night it’s hard not to feel defeated. It’s definitely not just work, although work is usually where it starts. Like I’ll be lying there and first it’s, “Are we actually going to hit the launch numbers?” or “Did I miss something in the forecast?” And then I’ll think about someone on my team who seemed a little off that day and start wondering if they’re unhappy or about to quit.

Yes, it is. I think I used to tell myself it was just a bad night here and there, and I could sort of power through it, but now I notice I’m almost planning my whole day around the assumption that I’m going to be up at 3 or 4.

At work, it’s made me much more hesitant than I used to be. I’ll go into pipeline reviews and instead of just making the call or trusting the numbers, I keep second-guessing myself out loud, or I’ll ask for one more check, one more scenario, one more clarification. And I can feel the room getting tense sometimes, because I’m probably making everyone else feel like nothing is solid enough. It’s not that I can’t do my job, but decisions that should be fairly straightforward take so much more energy because I’m trying to prevent some problem I’m imagining later.

And then by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I don’t have much left. My husband will ask a normal question or want to talk about something, and I can be kind of short or reactive, not because he’s doing anything wrong, but because I feel like I’ve been bracing all day. I’m physically there, but I’m not really present the way I want to be. So yes, knowing I’m probably going to be awake again definitely affects how I move through the day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0, G47.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest reason I’m here is my sleep has gotten really unreliable. Like, I’ll be completely exhausted by the end of the day and think, “Okay, I’m definitely going to pass out tonight,” but then I get into bed and my mind just… starts running. I’m replaying sales forecasts, conversations with my team, things I should have said differently, issues I need to fix, all of it.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and it’s like my brain turns back on immediately. I can’t get it to power down again, even though I know I need to sleep. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to feel like I can’t count on getting a normal night’s rest anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind racing and sleep issues are affecting your mood or energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, I’m still getting through the day, so it’s not like I’ve completely fallen apart, but it’s started to affect things in ways I can’t really ignore anymore.\n\nAt work, I’m second-guessing myself constantly. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my day is decisions, forecasts, pipeline reviews, talking through risks with the team, and I used to be able to trust my read on things more. Now I’ll go into a pipeline review and I’ve already mentally replayed the numbers a dozen times, but I’m still questioning whether I’m missing something or if I should have pushed harder on a deal or if I’m about to make the wrong call. And I think the team can feel that tension from me. Those meetings have gotten more strained because I’m not just reviewing the pipeline, I’m sort of… interrogating everything.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I feel completely used up. Then I feel bad about that too, which just gives me more to think about later when I’m trying to sleep. So yes, it’s affecting my energy, but also my patience and how available I am to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your mind starts racing?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s usually when it feels the loudest, honestly. During the day I can at least tell myself I’m doing something about it — checking numbers, answering emails, talking to someone on my team — but once I’m lying there, there’s nothing to do except think.\n\nAnd it jumps around so fast. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think. I wish I could point to one clean thing and say, “That’s the problem,” but it doesn’t really work like that.\n\nMost days my mind just jumps from one worry to the next. I’ll start with whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then I’m suddenly thinking about whether someone on my team has seemed off or quiet and maybe they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then it’s the mortgage, or a client renewal that still hasn’t come through, or some small delay in an email response that probably means nothing, but in my head it starts turning into this whole bigger problem.\n\nIt’s like every loose end feels dangerous, even if logically I know some of them are normal. I can tell myself, “Okay, this is just a delay,” or “You don’t actually know that anything is wrong,” but then my brain immediately goes, “But what if it is?” And that’s the part that keeps me awake — not one specific crisis, just this constant scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're consistently waking up at the same time every night, such as 3 or 4 in the morning, regardless of when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Not every single night like clockwork, but a lot of nights I do wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s this awful feeling because I’ll still be exhausted, but my brain is suddenly just… on.\n\nI can go to bed completely drained, and then either I can’t fall asleep because I’m replaying forecasts or thinking through team issues, or I finally do fall asleep and then wake up in that early-morning window. And once I’m awake, it’s like my mind immediately picks up right where it left off — launch numbers, whether I missed something, whether someone on my team is frustrated, what I need to fix the next day. I’ll look at the clock and think, “Please, not again,” because I know if I start engaging with those thoughts I’m going to be up for a while.\n\nSo yes, that 3 or 4 a.m. waking has definitely become part of it, and it’s one of the main reasons I’m here. My sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more alert or awake at any specific time of the day, aside from when you wake up in the early morning hours?\n\nPatient: Not really in a good way, no. I wouldn’t say there’s a time of day where I feel naturally more alert and clear, like I’ve rested and I’m ready to go.\n\nIt’s more that even when I go to bed exhausted, my brain won’t shut off. I’ll be lying there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and then if I do fall asleep, I often wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind immediately powers back on. Not in a productive, awake way — more like I can’t get it to power down again.\n\nDuring the day I’m functioning, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling especially alert at a certain time. It’s more like I’m tired, but my thoughts keep running anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your sleep patterns or the times you wake up have changed significantly with the seasons or when you travel across time zones?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really seasonal or tied to travel, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s not like winter comes and suddenly my sleep shifts, or I cross a time zone and that explains it.\n\nIt feels much more connected to this pattern I’ve gotten into where my sleep just isn’t reliable anymore. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, I’ll lie there and my mind starts replaying things — forecasts, numbers, team issues, whether I missed something — and then if I do fall asleep, I’ll often wake up around 3 or 4 and I can’t get my brain to power down again.\n\nSo the timing is pretty consistent in that way, but I wouldn’t say it changes with seasons or travel. It’s more like my brain has learned this awful routine.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in your life that you think might be triggering these nighttime worries and wake-ups, or does it seem unrelated to any specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: I do think there’s a trigger, or at least a starting point. It’s hard to say it’s one single event, because it’s been kind of gradual, but this really started building after the sales division reorganization about thirteen months ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stress, like everyone was unsettled and there were new reporting lines and expectations, and I figured once things settled down I’d settle down too. But instead it felt like my mind never really came out of that mode. Since then, the nighttime worrying and waking up has just become more and more baked into my routine.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels connected to that reorganization and everything that shifted after it. I don’t know if that’s the whole explanation, but that’s when I can trace it back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the sales division reorganization is still causing you significant stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think about it every single second in those exact words — “the reorganization” — but it feels like everything has been building from that point. It was about thirteen months ago now, and at the time I thought it was temporary, like once the new structure became normal, I’d feel normal again too.\n\nBut it hasn’t really felt that way. It’s like the stress from that period never fully let up, and instead it sort of accumulated. So yes, I’d say it’s still significant, even if it’s less like one dramatic event and more like this ongoing thing that started back then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress from the sales division reorganization still the main thing that's on your mind when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: It’s part of it, but honestly, it’s not just the reorganization itself anymore. When I’m lying there awake, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next before I even realize it’s happening.\n\nLike I’ll start thinking about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, and then that turns into worrying that maybe I missed something in the forecast, and then suddenly I’m thinking about someone on my team seeming quieter than usual and wondering if they’re about to quit. And then somehow it’s the mortgage, or whether a client renewal is going to fall through, or some email I didn’t answer fast enough.\n\nIt’s like everything feels connected and important at 3 in the morning. Even a small delay or something that should be manageable starts feeling like it could turn into a much bigger problem. So I guess the reorganization is still the backdrop, but at night it’s more like my brain uses that as the starting point and then runs through every possible thing that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel sad or depressed when you wake up in the middle of the night and can't fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think sad or depressed is usually the main feeling, no. I mean, I’m not waking up and crying or feeling hopeless in that moment, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… I wake up and I’m immediately frustrated and tense because I can tell my brain is already starting up again. Sometimes I do feel kind of low in the sense of, “Great, here we go again, I’m going to be exhausted tomorrow,” but I wouldn’t describe it as this deep sadness. It’s more discouraging, maybe, and tiring.\n\nI guess there are moments where I feel worn down by it, because when it keeps happening night after night it’s hard not to feel defeated. But when I first wake up, the main thing is usually worry and irritation that I can’t just turn it off and go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying awake at night, are your worries mostly about work or are they about other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work, although work is usually where it starts. Like I’ll be lying there and first it’s, “Are we actually going to hit the launch numbers?” or “Did I miss something in the forecast?” And then I’ll think about someone on my team who seemed a little off that day and start wondering if they’re unhappy or about to quit.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay contained to that. It’ll jump to the mortgage, or whether a client renewal might fall through, or some small delay that happened earlier and suddenly it feels like it could turn into this much bigger problem. It’s like my brain treats everything as connected, so one work worry turns into money, then staffing, then clients, then home stuff.\n\nMost days it feels like that pattern is there in some form. I know some of the individual things are normal things to think about, but at night they don’t feel normal-sized. They feel like they could spiral if I don’t somehow solve them right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your daytime behavior or activities are affected in any way by the fact that you know you'll likely be awake at 3 or 4 in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I think I used to tell myself it was just a bad night here and there, and I could sort of power through it, but now I notice I’m almost planning my whole day around the assumption that I’m going to be up at 3 or 4.\n\nAt work, it’s made me much more hesitant than I used to be. I’ll go into pipeline reviews and instead of just making the call or trusting the numbers, I keep second-guessing myself out loud, or I’ll ask for one more check, one more scenario, one more clarification. I don’t know that I’d say “empty,” exactly. That word feels a little… I don’t know, more blank than what I usually feel. During the day I can feel worn out or discouraged, especially if I’m already tired or I’ve been stuck in my head for hours, but I’m not walking around feeling numb or hollow all day.

Sad is kind of similar. I have moments where I feel down, like, “Why can’t I just handle things normally?” or I’ll feel upset with myself if I’ve been tense or short with someone. But it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness that’s there no matter what. It’s more like I get weighed down by the stress of everything, and then that can look or feel a little sad from the outside, maybe.

So… no, not usually sad or empty in a big, ongoing way. More overwhelmed, tired, and frustrated with myself.

I’m replaying sales forecasts, conversations with my team, things I should have said differently, issues I need to fix, all of it.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning and it’s like my brain turns back on immediately. I can’t get it to power down again, even though I know I need to sleep. I’ll start with, like, whether we’re actually going to hit the launch numbers, and then suddenly I’m wondering if someone on my team seemed quiet because they’re unhappy or about to quit. Then I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal that hasn’t come through yet, or some tiny delay in a response that probably means nothing but in my head becomes this whole chain of problems.\n\nIt’s not even one single worry I can pin down. It’s more like my brain treats every loose end as if it could turn into something major, and at night I can’t seem to talk myself out of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it comes on suddenly and peaks really fast within a few minutes. I mean, I definitely get anxious, and sometimes it feels intense, but it’s usually more like it builds from thinking and overthinking rather than hitting me out of nowhere.\n\nLike, if I’m lying there at night or something stressful is happening at work, I can feel myself getting more wound up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had those sudden episodes where it’s like a wave of fear just takes over all at once. At least, not that I’ve recognized that way.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out lately, or is it more about everything in general that's keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: It’s more everything in general, I think.
