Like I’ll be lying there and first it’s, “Are we actually going to hit the launch numbers?” or “Did I miss something in the forecast?” And then I’ll think about someone on my team who seemed a little off that day and start wondering if they’re unhappy or about to quit.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay contained to that. It’ll jump to the mortgage, or whether a client renewal might fall through, or some small delay that happened earlier and suddenly it feels like it could turn into this much bigger problem. It’s like my brain treats everything as connected, so one work worry turns into money, then staffing, then clients, then home stuff.\n\nMost days it feels like that pattern is there in some form. I know some of the individual things are normal things to think about, but at night they don’t feel normal-sized. They feel like they could spiral if I don’t somehow solve them right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your daytime behavior or activities are affected in any way by the fact that you know you'll likely be awake at 3 or 4 in the morning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I think I used to tell myself it was just a bad night here and there, and I could sort of power through it, but now I notice I’m almost planning my whole day around the assumption that I’m going to be up at 3 or 4.\n\nAt work, it’s made me much more hesitant than I used to be. I’ll go into pipeline reviews and instead of just making the call or trusting the numbers, I keep second-guessing myself out loud, or I’ll ask for one more check, one more scenario, one more clarification. And I can feel the room getting tense sometimes, because I’m probably making everyone else feel like nothing is solid enough. It’s not that I can’t do my job, but decisions that should be fairly straightforward take so much more energy because I’m trying to prevent some problem I’m imagining later.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I don’t have much left. My husband will ask a normal question or want to talk about something, and I can be kind of short or reactive, not because he’s doing anything wrong, but because I feel like I’ve been bracing all day. I’m physically there, but I’m not really present the way I want to be. So yes, knowing I’m probably going to be awake again definitely affects how I move through the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel sad or empty when you're awake during the day, not just when you wake up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “empty,” exactly. That word feels a little… I don’t know, more blank than what I usually feel. During the day I can feel worn out or discouraged, especially if I’m already tired or I’ve been stuck in my head for hours, but I’m not walking around feeling numb or hollow all day.\n\nSad is kind of similar. I have moments where I feel down, like, “Why can’t I just handle things normally?” or I’ll feel upset with myself if I’ve been tense or short with someone. But it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness that’s there no matter what. It’s more like I get weighed down by the stress of everything, and then that can look or feel a little sad from the outside, maybe.\n\nSo… no, not usually sad or empty in a big, ongoing way. More overwhelmed, tired, and frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually wake up feeling refreshed, or do you often wake up feeling unrefreshed or groggy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I almost never wake up feeling refreshed lately. Even if I go to bed completely exhausted, like I’m sure I should just pass out, my brain doesn’t really shut off. I’ll lie there replaying forecasts, thinking through team issues, wondering if I missed something, and it can take forever to fall asleep.\n\nAnd then a lot of nights I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and it’s like I’m instantly alert in the worst way. Not rested-alert, more like my brain picks up exactly where it left off. I’ll try to tell myself it’s not the time to solve anything, but I can’t get it to power down again. So when morning comes, I usually feel groggy and kind of behind before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your situation when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure that’s the exact word I’d use. When I’m lying there awake, it can definitely feel discouraging, like, “Here we go again,” and I’ll get frustrated because I know the next day is going to be harder if I can’t fall back asleep. Sometimes it feels very stuck in that moment, like I can’t figure out how to stop the cycle.\n\nBut I don’t think I’m lying there thinking there’s no point or that nothing can ever get better. It’s more that I feel trapped in the immediate problem — like my mind won’t let me rest, and I’m annoyed and worried about what it’s going to cost me the next day. So… not hopeless exactly. More overwhelmed and fed up, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime worries and waking up are related to feelings of anxiety or fear about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, it doesn’t always feel like fear in a dramatic way, like I’m lying there terrified, but it’s definitely this future-oriented worry. My mind starts jumping from one thing to the next — whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers, whether someone on my team is unhappy and might quit, whether a client renewal is going to fall through, or even stuff at home like the mortgage.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that it doesn’t stay contained. One small delay or one unanswered email can suddenly feel like the beginning of some much bigger problem. Like if a number is off, then I start thinking about what that means for the whole quarter, and then what my boss will think, and then whether I should have caught something sooner. It just kind of snowballs.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the waking up and the worrying are related to anxiety about what might happen, or what I might not be prepared for. It’s like my brain is trying to solve tomorrow at three in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty falling asleep, staying asleep, or both, on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: Both, unfortunately. Falling asleep is hard even when I’m exhausted — I’ll get into bed thinking I should be out immediately, and then my mind starts replaying work stuff, like forecasts or team issues, and I can’t seem to shut it down.\n\nAnd then staying asleep is a problem too. A lot of nights I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and once I’m awake, my brain just turns right back on. It’s not like I wake up briefly and roll over. I’ll lie there trying to calm myself down, but I can’t really get my mind to power down again. It’s been happening regularly enough that my sleep just feels really unreliable now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed during the daytime, not just when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. During the day I can definitely feel worn down, especially after a bad night, and I can get kind of discouraged or quieter than usual. But it doesn’t usually feel like this heavy sadness sitting on me all day.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense, preoccupied, and tired, if that makes sense. I’ll be going through meetings or emails and part of me is still thinking about what I didn’t sleep through, or what I need to fix next. So my mood isn’t great, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as depressed. More drained and on edge than sad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
